  def-bounded-c-one-domain-boundary-charts-and-outward-normal: mathematical-premise
  def-cm-continuum-motion: mathematical-premise
  def-ck-euclidean-maps-and-diffeomorphisms: mathematical-premise
  def-countable-choice: mathematical-premise
provenance:
  statement: ai-altered
  proof: ai-altered
proof_strategy: direct
physical_scope: "Conditional on the adopted force model and balance principles: on every bounded spatial region inside the current configuration the continuous traction field is represented by a unique continuous stress matrix, the reversed-normal identity t(-n) = -t(n) holds, and the couple-free torque balance forces the stress matrix to be symmetric. The result is a conditional deduction from the adopted balance principles and the cited mathematical Cauchy-tetrahedron and angular-balance theorems; it asserts no positivity, no constitutive relation and no measured stress."
sources:
  references:
    - title: "R. Abeyaratne, Continuum Mechanics, Volume II of Lecture Notes on the Mechanics of Solids (MIT, 1988, revised 2020), Sections 4.3-4.5 (Cauchy's tetrahedron, existence of the stress tensor and symmetry from angular momentum)"
      url: "https://web.mit.edu/abeyaratne/Volumes/RCA_Vol_II.pdf"
    - title: "K. Likharev, Essential Graduate Physics, Classical Mechanics (September 1, 2026 complete volume), Section 7.2 (the stress tensor, its symmetry from the torque balance, Eq. (7.25))"
      url: "https://essentialgraduatephysics.org/Files/CM%20merged.pdf"
verification:
  precheck: pass
---
Assume the Axiom of Countable Choice $\mathrm{AC}_\omega$
([[def-countable-choice]]), as used by the surface and divergence machinery of
the cited theorems. Let the model and its fields be as in
[[post-cm17-momentum-balance]]: a motion $\chi$ with current regions
$\Omega_s$, a continuous density field $\rho$, spatial velocity $u$ and
material acceleration $a=D_tu$, a continuous body-force field $b$ and a
continuous traction field $t$, with the balance principles (F) and (T) of
that postulate holding for every admissible region at every time $s$.
The stress matrix is continuous and unique per time; positivity of its
isotropic part, a constitutive relation and any measured stress are **not**
asserted.
[F1] Field regularity and force description ([[post-cm17-momentum-balance]], [[post-cm17-contact-force-model]], [[def-cm17-density-and-mass]], [[def-cm17-traction-and-stress]], [[def-cm-euclidean-spatial-calculus]]): $\rho$, $u$ and $a=D_tu$ are continuous and $b$ is continuous on the swept region domain; the traction field $t$ is of class $C^1$, so each $y\mapsto t(s,y,e_j)$ is $C^1$; and $t(s,y,n)$ is force per unit area depending on the surface at $(s,y)$ only through $n$.
[F3] Cauchy tetrahedron ([[thm-cm-traction-is-given-by-a-stress-matrix]]): if $O$ is open, $t:O\times S^2\to\mathbb R^3$ is continuous, $g:O\to\mathbb R^3$ is continuous and bounded with $C_g<\infty$, and $\int_{\partial D}t(y,\nu_D)\,dS+\int_Dg\,dy=0$ for every bounded piecewise $C^1$ Euclidean domain with a specified finite presentation whose closure and pieces lie in $O$ ([[def-bounded-piecewise-c-one-euclidean-domain]]), then there is a unique continuous $\sigma:O\to M_3(\mathbb R)$ with $t(y,n)=\sigma(y)n$, and $t(y,-n)=-t(y,n)$.
[F4] Couple-free symmetry ([[thm-cm-stress-matrix-is-symmetric]]): if in addition $\sigma$ is $C^1$, $b_0:O\to\mathbb R^3$ is continuous and for every bounded $C^1$ domain $D$ with $\overline D\subseteq O$ ([[def-bounded-c-one-domain-boundary-charts-and-outward-normal]]) one has $\int_{\partial D}\sigma\nu\,dS+\int_Db_0\,dy=0$ and $\int_{\partial D}y\times(\sigma\nu)\,dS+\int_Dy\times b_0\,dy=0$, then $\sigma(y)^{\mathsf T}=\sigma(y)$ for every $y\in O$.
2.1 Existence, uniqueness and the reversed-normal identity: step 1.1 provides exactly the hypotheses of [F3] with $O:=W$ and the continuous traction field $y\mapsto t(s,y,\cdot)$; hence there is a unique continuous $\sigma(s,\cdot):W\to M_3(\mathbb R)$ with $t(s,y,n)=\sigma(s,y)n$ on $W\times S^2$, and $t(s,y,-n)=-t(s,y,n)$ holds there. [step 1.1, F3]
4.1 Conclusion: steps 2.1 and 3.1 give the existence, uniqueness, reversed-normal identity and symmetry of the stress matrix on every bounded open $W$ with closure in the field domain at every time $s$; no positivity, constitutive relation, boundary condition or measured value is asserted. [step 2.1, step 3.1] ∎
- **Where symmetry comes from.** Symmetry is a consequence of the couple-free
  torque balance with the same volume force density as the force balance, not
  of the definition of traction; a model with couple stresses has a
  non-symmetric stress matrix and is excluded by
  [[post-cm17-momentum-balance]]. The constitutive items below produce
  symmetric matrices by construction, which is consistent with the present
  conclusion.
- **Uniqueness is per admissible region.** The matrix is unique on each $W$
  because the columns $\sigma(y)e_j$ are exactly $t(y,e_j)$; different
  bounded regions give consistent restrictions because both represent the
  same traction field.

- **Finite-time breakdown is not a flaw of the formulation.** By
  [[pthm-cm02-continuation]], a maximal motion can end at a finite time only by
  leaving every compact subset of its open state domain: by unbounded state,

  companion singular-collision counterexample exhibits both alternatives at
  once for an inverse-square external model.

FILE rem-cm06-dalembert-vakonomic-distinction
---
id: rem-cm06-dalembert-vakonomic-distinction
kind: remark
title: "The d'Alembert and vakonomic prescriptions are different models: what is proved, what is adopted, and which questions are open"
status: draft
origin: session
domain: physics
dependency_level: 11
deps: [def-cm06-holonomic-constraint-model, def-cm06-nonholonomic-model, post-cm06-ideal-reaction-law, def-cm-nonholonomic-velocity-constraint-law, cex-cm-dalembert-and-vakonomic-evolutions-differ, prop-cm-nonholonomic-distribution-is-not-tangent-to-level-sets, def-cm01-units]
dependency_roles:
  def-cm06-holonomic-constraint-model: formulation-prerequisite
  def-cm06-nonholonomic-model: formulation-prerequisite
  post-cm06-ideal-reaction-law: physical-assumption
  def-cm-nonholonomic-velocity-constraint-law: mathematical-premise
  cex-cm-dalembert-and-vakonomic-evolutions-differ: mathematical-premise
  prop-cm-nonholonomic-distribution-is-not-tangent-to-level-sets: mathematical-premise
  def-cm01-units: formulation-prerequisite
provenance:
  statement: ai-altered
  proof: not-applicable
physical_scope: "Scope remark distinguishing the two prescriptions for a velocity-constrained model: the d'Alembert (Chetaev, frozen-kernel) law adopted by this framework's nonholonomic models and the vakonomic (stationary augmented action) prescription, with the proved discrepancy for the model z'=xy', the exclusion of silent constraint substitution, and the open questions of rank and contact changes, rolling models and empirical adequacy. The remark asserts no measured value and no experimental comparison."
sources:
  references:
    - title: "G. J. Sussman and J. Wisdom, Structure and Interpretation of Classical Mechanics, second edition (MIT Press, 2014/2015), Section 1.10.3: the explicit statement that d'Alembert's ideal constraint forces do not generally equal the equations obtained by adding lambda times the constraint to an unconstrained variational action"
      url: "https://mitp-content-server.mit.edu/books/content/sectbyfn/books_pres_0/9579/sicm_edition_2.zip"
    - title: "J. Cortes, M. de Leon, D. Martin de Diego and S. Martinez, Geometric description of vakonomic and nonholonomic dynamics. Comparison of solutions (SIAM J. Control Optim., author PDF), Sections 2 and 5: the vakonomic problem and the Chetaev/d'Alembert equations for the same linear constraint, and the comparison of their solutions"
      url: "http://terrano.ucsd.edu/jorge/publications/data/2003_CoLeMaMa-sicon.pdf"
    - title: "D. Cline, Variational Principles in Classical Mechanics (2nd edition, University of Rochester River Campus Libraries, open textbook, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0), Sections 5.7 and 6.9: constrained variational systems, the straight-rolling exception, and applications involving non-holonomic constraints with the warning about variational substitutions"
      url: "https://downloads.libretexts.org/api/v1/download/phys-9557/pdf"
    - title: "D. Tong, Classical Dynamics (Cambridge lecture notes, January 2015 printing), Section 2.3.2, printed pages 20-21 (PDF pages 26-27): velocity-dependent constraints that cannot be integrated, the rolling coin, and the statement that no general method covers them"
      url: "https://www.davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/classical-dynamics/clas.pdf"
    - title: "I. Stewart, Advanced Classical Mechanics (MIT 8.09/8.309 Fall 2014 course text), printed page 25 (PDF page 28): a nonintegrable velocity constraint uses d'Alembert dynamics, and unconstrained stationary action with the velocity constraint inserted is a different problem"
      url: "https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-09-classical-mechanics-iii-fall-2014/d9bac33f6c60b304dc0398e99b327102_MIT8_09F14_full.pdf"
verification:
  precheck: n/a
---
For a velocity-constraint datum $(O_{v},k',A,b)$ of
[[def-cm06-nonholonomic-model]] the framework's model is the **d'Alembert
model**: admissible curves satisfy $Ax'+b=0$, and the reaction is the ideal
(Chetaev) reaction, annihilating the frozen virtual displacements
$\ker A(t,x(t))$ at each instant, equivalently $R=A^{\mathsf T}\mu$ for a
unique multiplier. The **vakonomic** prescription of
[[def-cm-nonholonomic-velocity-constraint-law]] is a different construction:
one forms the augmented functional
$$\Phi(q,\eta)=\int_{a}^{b}\Bigl[\tfrac12|q'|^{2}+\eta\,(z'-xy')\Bigr]dt$$
for the sample constraint $z'=xy'$ and a multiplier function $\eta$, and asks
for pairs $(q,\eta)$ stationary under variations of both the curve and the
multiplier. The two prescriptions are definitions of two different laws for
the same constraint; neither is a theorem about the other, and the framework
adopts the d'Alembert one for its nonholonomic models.
The distinction is not a matter of taste: for the constraint $z'=xy'$ the
d'Alembert and vakonomic motions from the same initial data differ.
[[cex-cm-dalembert-and-vakonomic-evolutions-differ]] proves this by
exhibiting a d'Alembert trajectory $\bigl(x(t),y(t),z(t)\bigr)$ with
$x'(0)=y'(0)=1$, $z'(0)=0$ from the origin and showing that its
$x''=0$ would force the vakonomic multiplier $\eta$ to vanish on an interval,
which would in turn force $y''=0$, contradicting the explicit d'Alembert
solution. The same constraint is genuinely nonholonomic in the sense of
clause (d) of [[def-cm06-nonholonomic-model]]:
[[prop-cm-nonholonomic-distribution-is-not-tangent-to-level-sets]] proves
that its distribution is not tangent to the level sets of any $C^{2}$ function
with nonvanishing differential. The conclusions are mathematical statements
about the two prescriptions; they are read into this page's model through the
definitions, with the model's status and exclusions of clause (e) carried
along.
The ideal (d'Alembert) condition for the holonomic branch is the adopted
postulate [[post-cm06-ideal-reaction-law]], and the corresponding ideal
condition for velocity constraints is built into the definition of the
d'Alembert model. In particular, no variational principle is claimed for the
nonholonomic models of this page: inserting the velocity constraint into a
Lagrangian and varying without constraint is the vakonomic construction and
generally produces different equations, and substituting the constraint into
the kinetic energy of a constrained system is justified only in the
holonomic-equivalent case, where the substitution parametrizes the position
locus rather than imposing a velocity law. The sources record both warnings
(SICM §1.10.3; Stewart, printed page 25; Cline §§5.7 and 6.9; Cortes et al.).
The following are outside this page and remain open here: the behaviour of
either prescription at a point where the rank of $A$ drops; unilateral
constraints and contact-mode changes (a body that leaves a surface or begins
to slip), which require mode-change models with impact or friction laws; the
determination of which prescription describes a given physical mechanism; and
the quantitative comparison of either model with reported observations.
Rolling is a family of models rather than one model: the planar no-slip law
used on the companion page is holonomic-equivalent, the straight-rolling
exception is noted in the sources, and general rolling constraints are
reported nonholonomic without a local proof on this page. Friction,
rolling resistance, material deformation and the ability of a contact to
supply the required reaction are application-level premises; no measured
value and no empirical adequacy claim is made by any item of this page.

      url: "https://downloads.libretexts.org/api/v1/download/phys-9557/pdf"

## Remarks
- **Velocity constraints are not covered by the action principle.** The
  reduction theorem of this page applies to ideal *holonomic* constraints. For
  a genuine velocity constraint the unrestricted minimisation of the action of
  the substituted Lagrangian, or of an augmented action with multipliers, is a
  different law from the d'Alembert equation of the preceding page:
  [[def-cm-nonholonomic-velocity-constraint-law]] defines both prescriptions
  for the constraint $z'=xy'$ and
  [[cex-cm-dalembert-and-vakonomic-evolutions-differ]] proves that their
  evolutions differ from the same initial data. Therefore the model's
  nonholonomic branch is not obtained by "imposing a constraint inside the
  action", and the examples page quantifies the discrepancy. Any identification
  of the two prescriptions requires its own justification and is not asserted
  here.

FILE rem-cm08-external-symmetry-breaking
- **Symmetries are properties of the model, not of the kinematics.** The
  symmetry criteria of this page are identities imposed on the model's
  declared mechanical Lagrangian $L=K-U$ and its residual forces
  ([[def-cm08-symmetry-of-the-mechanical-lagrangian]]); the affine structure
  of the spacetime, the availability of every inertial frame and the Galilean
  transformation law of the description are kinematic facts of the framework
  and do not by themselves make any potential invariant. Concretely, for the
  uniform-force model of the framework with potential
  $U=-\sum_{i}m_{i}\,\mathbf g\cdot x_{i}$ one has
  $\sum_{i}\nabla_{x_{i}}U=-M_{\mathrm{tot}}\mathbf g$, so the translation
  condition of [[pthm-cm08-translation]] holds exactly for directions
  $a\perp\mathbf g$; for every such model the momentum component along a
  direction with $a\cdot\mathbf g\ne0$ is not conserved, and the sharpness
  clauses of [[pthm-cm08-translation]] and [[pthm-cm08-boost-boundary]] locate
  the failure at the maximal motion through any state with $a\cdot\nabla U\ne0$.
  Likewise a confining external potential such as
  $U=\tfrac{k}{2}\sum_{i}\lvert x_{i}-x_{i}^{0}\rvert^{2}$ fails the
  translation condition at every configuration whose centre of mass differs
  from the reference configuration, and both the momentum and the boost charge
  of [[pthm-cm08-boost-boundary]] are then unavailable.

FILE rem-cm09-zero-modes-degeneracy-and-scope
A **zero mode** of a coupled quadratic model is an index with modal
stiffness $\lambda_{i}=0$ ([[def-cm09-coupled-quadratic-model]] clause (e));
the multiplicity of the value $0$ is $\dim\ker K$, and it counts the
independent directions in which the quadratic model exerts no restoring
force. By clause 2 of [[pthm-cm09-free-modes-and-degeneracy]] the free motion
in such a direction is affine, $z_{i}(t)=a_{i}+b_{i}t$, not oscillatory; for
$b_{i}\ne0$ it is unbounded on $[0,\infty)$. The classical instance is the
translation mode of a chain or molecule: the linearisation of a
translation-invariant model at a non-isolated minimum has a flat
second-order direction, and the "mode" of zero frequency is the uniform
motion of the whole system rather than a vibration. A constant generalized
force component along a zero mode produces quadratic growth in time by
clause 2(c) of [[pthm-cm09-forced-modal-response]], the degenerate analogue
of resonance; the model has no bounded steady response in that direction.
Zero modes are therefore excluded whenever a statement about oscillations
alone is intended: at a strict local minimum the stiffness is positive
semidefinite and $\ker K$ can be nontrivial even at an isolated strict minimum, as $U(q)=q^4$ at $0$ shows; the Lyapunov stability of
[[pthm-cm09-linearization-at-a-strict-minimum]] remains valid in that case
because it does not use positivity of the stiffness.
If a modal stiffness $\lambda$ has multiplicity $m>1$, the individual
eigenvectors and hence the individual modal vectors $u_{i}$ of a chosen
diagonalisation are **not** intrinsic: every orthonormal basis of the
eigenspace gives an equally valid decoupling. What is intrinsic is the
subspace $N_{\lambda}$ and its pure-$\lambda$ family of motions, exactly as
clause 4 of [[pthm-cm09-free-modes-and-degeneracy]] records: the pure-$\lambda$
motions are the solutions of $q''+\lambda q=0$ with values in
$N_{\lambda}$, a family with $2m$ parameters, and for $\lambda>0$ all of them
oscillate with the single frequency $\omega=\sqrt{\lambda}$. A statement
about "the $i$-th mode" must therefore be read as a statement about a chosen
basis vector, while the frequency, the subspace and the multi-parameter
family are the basis-independent objects. Resonance of a degenerate family
with a prescribed sinusoidal force is likewise basis-independent: it occurs
when $\Omega^2=\lambda$ and the modal force has a nonzero component in the corresponding modal eigenspace (equivalently, the mass-metric projection of $M^{-1}J_0$ onto $N_\lambda$ is nonzero)
(clause 2 and the remarks of [[pthm-cm09-forced-modal-response]]).

## Remarks
- **Constraints are covered only by elimination.** The only constraint
  statement of this page is clause 4 of
  [[pthm-cm12-hamilton-equivalence]], in which a scleronomic regular
  holonomic constraint is eliminated by a parametrization and the reaction
  never appears in the reduced equations. Constraint theory in phase space —
  multipliers adjoined to a Hamiltonian system, primary and secondary
  constraints, or any generalised constraint algorithm — is not defined,
  proved, or claimed on this page; the cited treatments likewise either
  restrict themselves to constraints imposed on Lagrangians or refer that
  subject elsewhere. In particular, the Lagrange-multiplier function of an
  ideal-reaction representation has a singular velocity Hessian in the
  ambient variables and is not itself a hyperregular presentation of this
  page, as the constraint items of the earlier pair record.

FILE rem-cm13-locality-topology
- **Determinant tests.** Canonicity of a $C^{1}$ map of the presentation is
  equivalent to the matrix identity
  $DC^{\mathsf T}J\,DC=J$ in canonical coordinates
  ([[def-cm13-canonical-map]],
  [[prop-cm-canonical-map-tests-and-hamiltonian-transport]]). The determinant
  test $|\det DC|=1$ is necessary but not sufficient in dimension $2\cdot3N
  \ge4$: the companion page records a volume-preserving shear of a four-
  dimensional phase domain that fails the fundamental bracket
  $\{Q^{2},P_{1}\}=0$; for a single degree of freedom the two tests coincide.

FILE rem-cm14-scope-of-hamilton-jacobi-and-integrability


FILE rem-cm15-model-boundaries


FILE rem-cm16-scope-of-stability-and-chaos
**1. Model deductions only.** Every theorem of the page is a conditional
deduction about the declared model class of
[[def-cm16-flow-equilibria-and-invariant-sets]]: a force model with stated
masses, configuration domain, regularity and — where relevant — periodicity.
The conclusions about equilibria, tori, horseshoes and recurrence are
properties of those models. None of them asserts that a physical system is
exactly such a model, and none carries a measured value: the constants
appearing in the statements (couplings, energy scales, pulse parameters, the
smallness thresholds $c$ and $\delta_0$) are model data, and no measurement,
calibration, sampling or uncertainty budget occurs anywhere on the page.
**2. Stability is not attraction, and one notion is not another.**
[[pthm-cm16-energy-stability-and-instability]] establishes Lyapunov stability
of a strict minimum, linear instability from a negative Hessian eigenvalue,
nonlinear instability from a strictly concave potential, and the absence of
asymptotic stability under exact energy conservation. The degenerate case (a
zero Hessian eigenvalue) and the mixed saddle case (a negative eigenvalue
together with oscillatory directions) are deliberately left open: only the
linear statement is made there. The harmonic-oscillator counterexample shows
that the page's stability vocabulary cannot be collapsed.

FILE rem-cm18-primary-report-contract
1. **Source.** A primary report with a resolvable reference, read in full:
   an apparatus paper, an instrument report or a data release, not a
   textbook exposition of a law and not a secondary anecdote.
2. **Setup.** The prepared system in the vocabulary of the model: labels,
   declared parameters, configurations and the initial-data region
   ([[def-cm18-observable-model]]); the apparatus and its settings; and the
   **calibration** with its reference inputs and their declared deviations
   ([[def-cm18-calibration]]).
3. **Procedure.** The measurement protocol in a declared inertial frame:
   observation times, readout, units; the **sampling model** with its
   adopted IID and second-moment hypotheses, or an explicit statement of the
   dependence or drift that replaces them ([[def-cm18-sampling-model]]); and
   the selection of the reported data.
4. **Observations.** The values actually reported by the source, with their
   units and their conditions, quoted or transcribed faithfully. If the
   source records no quantitative detail, only a correspondingly limited
   qualitative account is supported; no precision, count or value may be
   supplied from elsewhere.
5. **Uncertainty.** The statistical and systematic uncertainty treatment of
   the source and of the calibration: Type A evaluation from repeated
   observations, Type B evaluation from declared bounds and reference data,
   combination and expansion, and the deviations of the reference standards.
   Measured inputs of the comparison model carry their uncertainties and
   conditions into the conclusion ([[rem-cm-inference-and-uncertainty-scope]]).
6. **Interpretation.** A specified comparison between the reported
   observations and a specified prediction of the model, including the
   idealizations the prediction uses; a specified discrepancy measure; and a
   specified test — data space, model family, null set and rejection region
   with its size ([[def-cm-statistical-test-and-size]]) — or a specified
   estimation or confidence construction ([[def-cm-confidence-set-and-coverage]],
   [[def-cm-likelihood-function]]). Agreement is evidence for the joint
   assumptions of model, apparatus and analysis, and a discrepancy may
   expose any of them.

    - title: "W. Barsukow, Introduction to Conservation Laws: Theory and Numerics (lecture notes, TUM, Winter term 2021/22), Sections 1.1–1.2 (Eulerian fixed-volume and Lagrangian moving-volume descriptions of the same balance)"

[F2] Divergence theorem: for a bounded $C^1$ domain $C$ and $F\in C^1(\overline C;\mathbb R^3)$, $\int_C\operatorname{div}F=\int_{\partial C}F\cdot\nu\,dS$ ([[thm-divergence-theorem-for-bounded-c-one-euclidean-domains]]). Applied to $F=\rho(w-u)$ and to the rows $F_i=(\sigma_{i1},\sigma_{i2},\sigma_{i3})$, it gives $\int_C\operatorname{div}(\rho(w-u))=\int_{\partial C}\rho(w-u)\cdot\nu\,dS$ and $\int_Cg\,dx=\int_{\partial C}\sigma\nu\,dS$ ([[def-cm-traction-and-stress]]).
- The identities fix the inertial conventions: the momentum flux uses the spatial momentum density $\rho u$ times the normal component of the *relative* velocity, and the traction term is the surface integral of $\sigma\nu$. No stress symmetry, constitutive relation or boundary condition is used or asserted; the only regularities required are those stated.

Let $\rho:U\to(0,\infty)$ be a $C^1$ density field, let $\sigma:U\to M_3(\mathbb R)$ be a $C^1$ field (the stress field of [[def-cm-traction-and-stress]]), and let $b:U\to\mathbb R^3$ be continuous. Suppose:
[F2] Divergence theorem on a bounded $C^1$ domain: for $F\in C^1(\overline\Omega;\mathbb R^3)$, $\int_\Omega\operatorname{div}F\,dx=\int_{\partial\Omega}F\cdot\nu\,dS$, with interior-up-to-boundary regularity ([[thm-divergence-theorem-for-bounded-c-one-euclidean-domains]], [[def-bounded-c-one-domain-boundary-charts-and-outward-normal]]). Applied to the rows $F_i:=(\sigma_{i1},\sigma_{i2},\sigma_{i3})$ on $\Omega=\chi_t(B')$, whose closure lies in $U$, it gives $\int_{\partial\chi_t(B')}\sigma\nu\,dS=\int_{\chi_t(B')}g\,dx$ componentwise; the hypothesis (L) provides the surface integral with the outward normal convention of [[def-cm-traction-and-stress]].
- The row divergence convention $g_i=\sum_j\partial_j\sigma_{ij}$ is exactly the vector whose component $i$ appears in the divergence theorem applied to the $i$-th row of $\sigma$; it is the matrix divergence consistent with the traction convention $t(n)=\sigma n$ of [[def-cm-traction-and-stress]]. For a symmetric stress matrix the row and column divergences coincide; their agreement is not assumed here.

FILE thm-cm-traction-is-given-by-a-stress-matrix

id: thm-cm-traction-is-given-by-a-stress-matrix
kind: theorem
title: "A continuous traction field balanced on every piecewise $C^1$ region is given by a continuous stress matrix, and $t(-n)=-t(n)$"
status: draft
origin: pipeline
domain: mathematics
dependency_level: 1
deps: [def-cm-traction-and-stress, def-bounded-piecewise-c-one-euclidean-domain, def-surface-integral-on-a-compact-c-one-hypersurface, thm-divergence-theorem-for-bounded-piecewise-c-one-domains, def-ck-euclidean-maps-and-diffeomorphisms, def-euclidean-inner-product, def-metric-ball, thm-heine-borel-rn, thm-heine-cantor-metric, thm-extreme-value-metric, lem-cross-product-is-bilinear-alternating-and-orthogonal, thm-cross-product-norm-is-the-two-vector-gram-determinant, prop-order-and-scalar-rules-for-the-nonnegative-integral, thm-linearity-of-the-lebesgue-integral-on-l-one, def-integral-over-a-measurable-set, def-matrix-space, def-matrix-product-and-identity-matrix]

provenance:
  statement: ai-altered
  proof: ai-altered
proof_strategy: direct
sources:
  references:
    - title: "R. Abeyaratne, Continuum Mechanics, Volume II of Lecture Notes on the Mechanics of Solids (MIT, 1988, revised 2020), Section 4.3 (force, traction and Cauchy's hypothesis) and Section 4.5 (the Cauchy tetrahedron and the existence of the stress tensor)"
      url: "https://web.mit.edu/abeyaratne/Volumes/RCA_Vol_II.pdf"
    - title: "MIT OpenCourseWare 12.005, Applications of Continuum Mechanics, Lecture 3: tensors, stress, the Cauchy tetrahedron and Cauchy's formula"
      url: "https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/12-005-applications-of-continuum-mechanics-to-earth-atmospheric-and-planetary-sciences-spring-2006/5005ac6aec16d031bffbd361c792ca70_lec3.pdf"
**Given:** The open set $O$, the continuous traction field $t$, the bounded continuous field $g$, and the balance hypothesis (B).
3.1 (Signed tetrahedron identity.) Fix $n\in S^2$ with $n_i\ne0$ for all $i$ and put $s_i=\operatorname{sign}(n_i)$. For $0<h<r\min_i|n_i|/2$ the signed tetrahedron of [F3](2) has closure in $O$. Divide its balance (B) by the oblique-face area $A$. The coordinate-face area ratios are $|n_i|$, their actual outward normals are $-s_ie_i$, and the volume ratio is $h/3$. All face points tend to $y_0$ as $h\downarrow0$, so [F2] and the bound $\|\int_{T_h}g\|_2/A\le C_gh/3$ give $$t(y_0,n)+\sum_{i=1}^3|n_i|t(y_0,-s_ie_i)=0.$$ By (R), $t(y_0,-s_ie_i)=-s_it(y_0,e_i)$ for either sign of $s_i$. Since $|n_i|s_i=n_i$, this is $$t(y_0,n)=\sum_{i=1}^3n_i t(y_0,e_i). \tag{T}$$ [given, F1, F2, F3, step 1.1, step 2.1, algebra]
- If a body couple density or a surface couple field is present, the balance (B) must be modified and the reversed-normal conclusion can fail for the full traction; this item treats the couple-free pure-force balance only.
